*Replying to @fjzeit* ([link](https://x.com/fjzeit/status/2109205444912906732)): @voidcompiler well she did talk about mental models. i carried that through. might be my inference there, but i think those mental models are still relevant, although now we have the option of higher-level mental models when it's appropriate and responsible to adopt them.

> i actually intentionally left out maintainability/complexity as properties of good code. not that I don’t think they’re relevant, but they’re properties of the syntax not properties of the output, which are the 3 I landed on. it’s also weird for me to say this (as i do take care with my agents to write maintainable code), but my hypothesis is that it was only good because humans wrote the code. the real goal with those best practices was to make “code easy to change” (by humans). 
>
> well, agents, especially those running on the latest frontier models, can work in any codebase. code is easy to change! you can simply refactor and rewrite code trivially by just telling it to. therefore the only properties that matter are the runtime aspects: correctness, bug free, fast and efficient
>
> put another way: when agents write everything, does it matter if the code is concise or clean if the other 3 properties are true?

*Replying to @bendee983* ([link](https://x.com/bendee983/status/2109259204351381971)): Thought-provoking take on AI and coding. I would make one small correction. I think good code is:  1- Correct 2- Bug-free 3- Fast and cost-efficient to run 4- Well-architected  By no. 4 I mean it should have the right set of abstractions and hierarchies to be able to expand it in directions that matter. > not to mention that agents will continue getting more and more capable. my argument is that the definition of Good Code has changed

### 2026-10-11 15:50 UTC — [2109310646085107987](https://x.com/poteto/status/2109310646085107987)

Likes 3 · reposts 0 · replies 2 · bookmarks 1

*Replying to @Zhaowt95* ([link](https://x.com/Zhaowt95/status/2109308047109837061)): 智能体并没有让可维护性变得不重要，反而让它更关键了。 智能体在结构差的代码上更容易出错、消耗更多资源，也更难持续修改。研究显示，智能体基于自己生成的代码继续工作时，任务解决率最高下降约13%，因为会引入微妙的行为漂移（验证、错误处理等）。 现实数据更直接：AI让重复代码大幅上升、重构减少，代码量增加了，但软件产出没增加——审查和调试成了瓶颈。 “现在正确”不等于以后好改。结构差的代码会让智能体（和人类）更容易引入难以发现的回归。好结构让智能体更高效、更可靠，而不是相反。 优秀代码的定义变了，但可维护性的核心价值没有消失，反而被放大了。

> yeah but my point is that Good Code is correct, but free, fast and efficient on resources. what you’re saying is upstream of that. maintainability doesn’t actually 1:1 improve these, as sometimes you need to tradeoff vs performance. an agent can simply write low level imperative code if it needs to, even if it may be unreadable to humans

> well people are very social animals so we tend to think in terms of other people (this is my theory). it’s easier for the average person to hand off a task to their engineer agent, their client outreach agent, or an agent per customer they manage, or however their brain works. it’s just easier to group related tasks under a familiar concept and lets you multitask more effectively rather than you doing it yourself by piecing together multiple disparate threads
>
> it’s similar to Projects in that regard, the anthropomorphism just makes it easier to remember i think

*Replying to @SuperIronBob* ([link](https://x.com/SuperIronBob/status/2110209697441783993)): @LundukeJournal I'm not sure I agree with this, it's correct for most AI originated comments in code, and for most human comments. But for AI midtask the comments as ledger help with not repeating mistakes in this current push. And for future humans and AI a good comment has immense value.

> • comments go stale very quickly and confuse agents and people
> • they are often used by agents to explain workarounds that would've been better off actually fixing the code instead
> • they are not enforceable and waste your agents' tokens
>
> my point is that moving prose (comments, rules, agent.md, skills) into deterministic lint rules, better API design/types, is far more effective, enforceable, and token efficient.
>
> comments are simply a crutch that agents will happily misuse

*Replying to @taylorhbuilds* ([link](https://x.com/taylorhbuilds/status/2110497344235975107)): Hey I’ve got a question, been digging into pstack for a few days - is there a reason why you just use these all as skills instead of building them as like scripts or tool calls that run?   My biggest issue honestly I’m trying to work thru is efficiency - I assume you have all the usage in the world for grok/grokbot/models and I’m trying to shape them to fit the models and subscriptions I have and …

> i like the flexibility of skills since you can omit or invoke on demand! but in general i'm a big advocate of moving prose into deterministic code: lint rules, strong types, good API design are superior to comments, agents.md, rules, skills
>
> for pstack efficiency, definitely run /setup-pstack and choose your budget level, and when you invoke /poteto-mode you can also tell your agent not to create large swarms of subagents and it should remember that for the rest of the session. that's usually where the cost comes from

*Quoting @poteto* ([link](https://x.com/poteto/status/2110149626133438732)): ban and delete most if not all code comments!  agents love copying what already exists. a seemingly harmless comment explaining some temporary workaround is one of the worst viruses you can have in your codebase. like a weed it'll spread quickly as a tool for agents to quickly get their job done. before you know it, your codebase is overrun with hacks and stale comments. just one more workaround b…

*Replying to @poteto* ([link](https://x.com/poteto/status/2110758136852385984)): here are some power pstack prompts you can try to upgrade your code, starting with this one (prompt is at the bottom of the post)  refactoring to make your code friendlier to agents https://t.co/Fas1O67i4V

> comments are unstructured and unenforceable text. agents do read them but they are often ignored, go out of date, and gives your agent an escape hatch to do a bandaid, hack or a workaround instead. 
>
> use this prompt to audit your code comments to find opportunities to improve your code through stronger types, better API design, and create lint rules so the comments aren’t necessary!
>
